Yeah, thanks. I guess what finally pushed me to come in is… I can’t seem to keep all the moving parts organized anymore. No, it definitely wasn’t just recent. I mean, I can make it sound like it’s because work got more complicated, and it has, but looking back… yeah, this was there when I was a kid too.

In grade school, teachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. I’d do the assignment and then somehow not turn it in, or I’d lose the homework folder before it made it back to school.

So, yeah, long before I was a teenager, that pattern was already there.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do get restless, especially on long design calls. It’s like there’s this pressure building up in my body to move, even if I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. I’ll pace around my office, or move from one chair to another, or kind of shift around a lot.

It’s funny, I actually set up my office thinking it would make me more focused—good chair, second chair by the window, standing desk mat I barely use correctly—but on those calls I end up rotating around the room like that’s somehow part of the process. So yes, I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls, but sitting still for a long meeting can be really hard.

Um… I’m not sure that’s the main thing for me. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a way people have pointed out.

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I mean… the place it’s been most obvious, or at least most embarrassing, is work. I know you asked outside of work, and I’m trying to think about that, but the thing that keeps coming to mind is that it’s started affecting how people see me professionally.

Like, I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing—not the final filing, but one of those internal steps where you’re supposed to have things lined up—and that was… not great. And then there was this architecture review that went badly. It was a difficult one anyway, lots of moving pieces, but I wasn’t on top of it the way I should’ve been, and afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I could really lead the larger project.

So, I guess the short answer is: yes, it causes real problems, but the biggest damage I can point to is my work reputation. That’s the part that’s been hard to ignore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I’m trying to think of a clean home example, like a bill or a dentist appointment, but honestly the pattern shows up more as everything bleeding together.

What finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ve got patent notes in one place, workshop prep in another, gallery deadlines—my partner is involved in some of that world too, so it sort of spills into personal life—and then team decisions sitting in Slack or email or wherever. And instead of doing the thing that actually matters next, I end up starting whatever is loudest or most urgent-feeling in the moment.

So at home it’s less like, “I forgot one specific bill,” and more like I’m surrounded by half-sorted notes and reminders and I can’t make a sane plan out of it. Then something personal gets neglected because I spent the evening chasing the wrong task.

Yeah… I’m trying to pin down one specific thing, and I keep coming back to the same mess, basically. Like at home, it’s not just “I forgot to take out the trash” or something simple. It’s that I’ll have all these separate threads—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, decisions from the team—and they’re all scattered in different places.

And then in the evening I’ll sit down meaning to handle, say, something related to the gallery or something my partner was expecting me to follow up on, but I’ll notice some other note or message that feels more urgent, and suddenly I’m reorganizing that instead. Or half-reorganizing it, really. Then the actual thing that mattered doesn’t get done.

So the problem is less one missed appointment and more that I can’t seem to organize the moving parts anymore. I end up doing whatever is loudest in front of me, not what actually matters, and then it spills into home stuff.

I know you’re asking for one clean example, and I’m… annoyingly, I’m still thinking in piles rather than one event.

The real thing is that I can’t organize the moving parts anymore. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of disorganization and distractibility also cause problems for you outside of work—like at home, in relationships, or managing personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the place it’s been most obvious, or at least most embarrassing, is work. I know you asked outside of work, and I’m trying to think about that, but the thing that keeps coming to mind is that it’s started affecting how people see me professionally.\n\nLike, I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing—not the final filing, but one of those internal steps where you’re supposed to have things lined up—and that was… not great. And then there was this architecture review that went badly. It was a difficult one anyway, lots of moving pieces, but I wasn’t on top of it the way I should’ve been, and afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I could really lead the larger project.\n\nSo, I guess the short answer is: yes, it causes real problems, but the biggest damage I can point to is my work reputation. That’s the part that’s been hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of how this disorganization or distractibility has caused a problem at home or in your personal life—like forgetting bills, appointments, or commitments to family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m trying to think of a clean home example, like a bill or a dentist appointment, but honestly the pattern shows up more as everything bleeding together.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ve got patent notes in one place, workshop prep in another, gallery deadlines—my partner is involved in some of that world too, so it sort of spills into personal life—and then team decisions sitting in Slack or email or wherever. And instead of doing the thing that actually matters next, I end up starting whatever is loudest or most urgent-feeling in the moment.\n\nSo at home it’s less like, “I forgot one specific bill,” and more like I’m surrounded by half-sorted notes and reminders and I can’t make a sane plan out of it. Then something personal gets neglected because I spent the evening chasing the wrong task.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific recent example, outside of work, where this distractibility or disorganization caused a problem—like something with your partner, a household task, or a personal commitment that got missed or messed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m trying to pin down one specific thing, and I keep coming back to the same mess, basically. Like at home, it’s not just “I forgot to take out the trash” or something simple. Then the actual thing that mattered doesn’t get done.\n\nSo the problem is less one missed appointment and more that I can’t seem to organize the moving parts anymore. I end up doing whatever is loudest in front of me, not what actually matters, and then it spills into home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall one specific instance—like forgetting a plan with your partner, missing a personal deadline, or failing to follow through on a household responsibility—where this disorganization actually caused a real consequence at home, separate from work?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for one clean example, and I’m… annoyingly, I’m still thinking in piles rather than one event.\n\nThe real thing is that I can’t organize the moving parts anymore. Yeah, I mean… yes, she has been frustrated. I’m not sure I can give you the exact quote or the one perfect incident, but the pattern is there.

It’s usually not that I don’t want to do the thing for her. It’s more like I’ve got all these moving pieces in my head and in different places—patent notes, workshop stuff, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and then I sit down intending to follow through on something that matters, and I get pulled into whatever feels loudest right then.

I’ll start the day thinking I know what needs to happen, and then an email or a message or some urgent-looking thing pulls me sideways, and by the end I’ve touched six things but finished the wrong one. That’s basically why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say you've struggled with this kind of distractibility and trouble prioritizing since you were a child, or is this really a new pattern that started more recently?\n\nPatient: No, it definitely wasn’t just recent. I mean, I can make it sound like it’s because work got more complicated, and it has, but looking back… yeah, this was there when I was a kid too.\n\nIn grade school, teachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. And I remember—well, my mom kept some of those report cards for years, in this big folder with school pictures and random certificates—but the comments were always kind of the same. I’d do the assignment and then somehow not turn it in, or I’d lose the homework folder before it made it back to school.\n\nSo, yeah, long before I was a teenager, that pattern was already there.\n\nDoctor: Besides the trouble organizing and staying focused, do you also notice things like feeling restless, fidgety, or having trouble sitting still, or acting impulsively without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get restless, especially on long design calls. It’s like there’s this pressure building up in my body to move, even if I’m trying to look like I’m paying attention. I’ll pace around my office, or move from one chair to another, or kind of shift around a lot.\n\nIt’s funny, I actually set up my office thinking it would make me more focused—good chair, second chair by the window, standing desk mat I barely use correctly—but on those calls I end up rotating around the room like that’s somehow part of the process. So yes, I wouldn’t say I’m bouncing off the walls, but sitting still for a long meeting can be really hard.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself interrupting others, blurting out responses before they finish speaking, or making quick decisions without fully thinking them through?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure that’s the main thing for me. I don’t think of myself as someone who’s constantly cutting people off, at least not in a way people have pointed out.\n\nI mean, in meetings I’ll sometimes want to jump in because the conversation is moving fast, and if I don’t say the thought then it sort of… anyway, that’s probably not what you’re asking. I try pretty hard to wait my turn, especially at work, because those conversations can get messy quickly.\n\nQuick decisions… I don’t know. I can overthink things too, so I wouldn’t say I’m always impulsive. I guess I’d need an example of what you mean.\n\nDoctor: Does this kind of disorganization and distractibility also cause problems for you outside of work—like at home, in relationships, or managing personal responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… the place it’s been most obvious, or at least most embarrassing, is work. I know you asked outside of work, and I’m trying to think about that, but the thing that keeps coming to mind is that it’s started affecting how people see me professionally.\n\nLike, I missed a checkpoint for a patent filing—not the final filing, but one of those internal steps where you’re supposed to have things lined up—and that was… not great. And then there was this architecture review that went badly. It was a difficult one anyway, lots of moving pieces, but I wasn’t on top of it the way I should’ve been, and afterward I got the sense that some collaborators were questioning whether I could really lead the larger project.\n\nSo, I guess the short answer is: yes, it causes real problems, but the biggest damage I can point to is my work reputation. That’s the part that’s been hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a specific example of how this disorganization or distractibility has caused a problem at home or in your personal life—like forgetting bills, appointments, or commitments to family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m trying to think of a clean home example, like a bill or a dentist appointment, but honestly the pattern shows up more as everything bleeding together.\n\nWhat finally pushed me to come in is that I can’t seem to organize all the moving parts anymore. I’ve got patent notes in one place, workshop prep in another, gallery deadlines—my partner is involved in some of that world too, so it sort of spills into personal life—and then team decisions sitting in Slack or email or wherever. And instead of doing the thing that actually matters next, I end up starting whatever is loudest or most urgent-feeling in the moment.\n\nSo at home it’s less like, “I forgot one specific bill,” and more like I’m surrounded by half-sorted notes and reminders and I can’t make a sane plan out of it. Then something personal gets neglected because I spent the evening chasing the wrong task.\n\nDoctor: Can you think of a specific recent example, outside of work, where this distractibility or disorganization caused a problem—like something with your partner, a household task, or a personal commitment that got missed or messed up?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I’m trying to pin down one specific thing, and I keep coming back to the same mess, basically. Like at home, it’s not just “I forgot to take out the trash” or something simple. It’s that I’ll have all these separate threads—patent notes, workshop prep, gallery deadlines, decisions from the team—and they’re all scattered in different places.\n\nAnd then in the evening I’ll sit down meaning to handle, say, something related to the gallery or something my partner was expecting me to follow up on, but I’ll notice some other note or message that feels more urgent, and suddenly I’m reorganizing that instead. Or half-reorganizing it, really. Then the actual thing that mattered doesn’t get done.\n\nSo the problem is less one missed appointment and more that I can’t seem to organize the moving parts anymore. I end up doing whatever is loudest in front of me, not what actually matters, and then it spills into home stuff.\n\nDoctor: Can you recall one specific instance—like forgetting a plan with your partner, missing a personal deadline, or failing to follow through on a household responsibility—where this disorganization actually caused a real consequence at home, separate from work?\n\nPatient: I know you’re asking for one clean example, and I’m… annoyingly, I’m still thinking in piles rather than one event.\n\nThe real thing is that I can’t organize the moving parts anymore. I’ll have patent notes over here, workshop prep somewhere else, gallery deadlines in another place, and team decisions buried in messages. And then at home, when I’m supposed to follow through on something that actually matters, I’ll start with whatever feels loudest in front of me instead.\n\nSo the consequence is that the important thing gets pushed aside—not because I don’t care, but because I never get the pieces sorted into a real plan. That’s honestly what pushed me to come in. It’s like I’m constantly reacting to the nearest thread instead of doing the thing I actually meant to do.\n\nDoctor: Let's try a different angle: has your partner ever told you they were frustrated, hurt, or upset because you forgot something you'd promised to do for them—regardless of whether you can recall the exact details right now?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… yes, she has been frustrated. I’m not sure I can give you the exact quote or the one perfect incident, but the pattern is there.\n\nIt’s usually not that I don’t want to do the thing for her. It’s more like I’ve got all these moving pieces in my head and in different places—patent notes, workshop stuff, gallery deadlines, team decisions—and then I sit down intending to follow through on something that matters, and I get pulled into whatever feels loudest right then.\n\nAnd then later it turns out the thing she needed from me was the thing I didn’t do. So… yes, she gets upset about that. It’s embarrassing, because from the outside it just looks like I didn’t care or didn’t listen, but inside it feels like I can’t get the pieces into an order that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy. Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing. Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation. Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. I mean, I can make it sound like it’s because work got more complicated, and it has, but looking back… yeah, this was there when I was a kid too.\n\nIn grade school, teachers would write stuff like I was bright, but I daydreamed a lot. I’d do the assignment and then somehow not turn it in, or I’d lose the homework folder before it made it back to school.\n\nSo, yeah, long before I was a teenager, that pattern was already there.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost. Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S005\",\"name\":\"Organization_Difficulty\",\"description\":\"Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking for a concrete example of disorganization or distractibility causing problems in personal life; the profile most directly relevant is difficulty organizing tasks and activities, especially managing many moving parts and starting whatever feels loudest instead of what matters.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }
